        - article [ref=e56]:

            - generic [ref=e64]:
              - 'heading "RNAi and genome editing of sugarcane: Progress and prospects" [level=1] [ref=e66]'
              - generic [ref=e67]:
                - link "Eleanor Brant" [ref=e68] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Brant%20E"[Author]
                - superscript [ref=e69]: "1"
                - text: ","
                - link "Evelyn Zuniga‐Soto" [ref=e70] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Zuniga%E2%80%90Soto%20E"[Author]
                - superscript [ref=e71]: "1"
                - text: ","
                - link "Fredy Altpeter" [ref=e72] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Altpeter%20F"[Author]
                - superscript [ref=e73]: 1,
                - superscript [ref=e74]: ✉
              - list [ref=e75]:
                - listitem [ref=e76]:
                  - button "▸ Author information" [ref=e77] [cursor=pointer]
                - listitem [ref=e78]:
                  - button "▸ Article notes" [ref=e79] [cursor=pointer]
                - listitem [ref=e80]:
                  - button "▸ Copyright and License information" [ref=e81] [cursor=pointer]
              - generic: External, opens in a new tab.
              - generic [ref=e82]:
                - text: "PMCID: PMC11886501 PMID:"
                - link "40051334" [ref=e83] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40051334/
          - region "Article content" [ref=e84]:
            - generic [ref=e85]:
              - generic [ref=e86]:
                - heading "SUMMARY" [level=2] [ref=e87]: SUMMARY
                - paragraph [ref=e88]:
                  - text: Sugarcane, which provides 80% of global table sugar and 40% of biofuel, presents unique breeding challenges due to its highly polyploid, heterozygous, and frequently aneuploid genome. Significant progress has been made in developing genetic resources, including the recently completed reference genome of the sugarcane cultivar R570 and pan‐genomic resources from sorghum, a closely related diploid species. Biotechnological approaches including RNA interference (RNAi), overexpression of transgenes, and gene editing technologies offer promising avenues for accelerating sugarcane improvement. These methods have successfully targeted genes involved in important traits such as sucrose accumulation, lignin biosynthesis, biomass oil accumulation, and stress response. One of the main transformation methods—biolistic gene transfer or
                  - emphasis [ref=e89]: Agrobacterium
                  - text: ‐mediated transformation—coupled with efficient tissue culture protocols, is typically used for implementing these biotechnology approaches. Emerging technologies show promise for overcoming current limitations. The use of morphogenic genes can help address genotype constraints and improve transformation efficiency. Tissue culture‐free technologies, such as spray‐induced gene silencing, virus‐induced gene silencing, or virus‐induced gene editing, offer potential for accelerating functional genomics studies. Additionally, novel approaches including base and prime editing, orthogonal synthetic transcription factors, and synthetic directed evolution present opportunities for enhancing sugarcane traits. These advances collectively aim to improve sugarcane's efficiency as a crop for both sugar and biofuel production. This review aims to discuss the progress made in sugarcane methodologies, with a focus on RNAi and gene editing approaches, how RNAi can be used to inform functional gene targets, and future improvements and applications.
                - paragraph [ref=e91]:
                  - strong [ref=e92]: "Keywords:"
                  - text: RNA interference, VIGS, SIGS, VIGE, gene editing, TALEN, CRISPR, synthetic transcription factors, bioenergy, oilcane, biomass oil, lignin, sucrose, energycane
              - generic [ref=e93]:
                - heading "Significance Statement" [level=2] [ref=e94]: Significance Statement
                - paragraph [ref=e95]:
                  - text: With its highly polyploid genome, sugarcane offers unique challenges and opportunities for genetic modification. This article evaluates how RNAi has set the stage for gene editing and how new RNAi approaches can streamline target gene validation and inform the desired co‐editing level of target alleles/copies in sugarcane. Novel approaches accelerating functional genomics studies, proving precision genome modifications, and directing
                  - emphasis [ref=e96]: in planta
                  - text: evolution of enzymes are also discussed.
              - generic [ref=e97]:
                - heading "INTRODUCTION" [level=2] [ref=e98]: INTRODUCTION
                - paragraph [ref=e99]:
                  - text: Sugarcane (
                  - emphasis [ref=e100]: Saccharum
                  - text: spp. hybrid) is cultivated across more than 110 countries on over 27 million hectares, providing 80% of global table sugar and 40% of global biofuel (OECD/FAO,
                  - link "2023" [ref=e101] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0154"
                    - superscript [ref=e102]: "2023"
                  - text: ). Genetic improvement of sugarcane faces significant challenges due to its complex highly polyploid, heterozygous, and frequently aneuploid genome (2
                  - emphasis [ref=e103]: "n"
                  - text: = 100–120), which originated from interspecific hybridization between
                  - emphasis [ref=e104]: Saccharum officinarum
                  - text: (2
                  - emphasis [ref=e105]: "n"
                  - text: = 80) and
                  - emphasis [ref=e106]: Saccharum spontaneum
                  - text: (2
                  - emphasis [ref=e107]: "n"
                  - text: = 36–128) (Healey et al.,
                  - link "2024" [ref=e108] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0070"
                  - text: ; Lovejot et al.,
                  - link "2017" [ref=e109] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0120"
                    - superscript [ref=e110]: "2017"
                  - text: ; Premachandran et al.,
                  - link "2011" [ref=e111] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0166"
                    - superscript [ref=e112]: "2011"
                  - text: ). Traditional breeding of new sugarcane cultivars is a lengthy process, typically requiring 12–15 years for parent identification, flowering synchronization, and progeny evaluation. The highly heterozygous nature of the genome necessitates vegetative propagation to maintain elite cultivar performance (Healey et al.,
                  - link "2024" [ref=e113] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0070"
                  - text: ; Hoang et al.,
                  - link "2015" [ref=e114] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0072"
                    - superscript [ref=e115]: "2015"
                  - text: ; Raboin et al.,
                  - link "2008" [ref=e116] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0170"
                    - superscript [ref=e117]: "2008"
                  - text: ; Wang et al.,
                  - link "2022" [ref=e118] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0217"
                    - superscript [ref=e119]: "2022"
                  - text: ).
                - paragraph [ref=e120]:
                  - text: Biotechnology approaches offer tremendous opportunities to accelerate sugarcane improvement, but they require knowledge of candidate gene sequence and function (Mohan et al.,
                  - link "2022" [ref=e121] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0140"
                  - text: ). While the large genome size (>10 Gb) and high frequency of sequence repeats initially hindered genome assembly efforts, several genetic resources are now available. These include the recently published complete reference genome of cultivar R570, which represents unique DNA sequences across approximately 12 chromosome copies (Healey et al.,
                  - link "2024" [ref=e122] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0070"
                  - text: ). Earlier resources include a mosaic monoploid genome of R570 (Garsmeur et al.,
                  - link "2018" [ref=e123] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0060"
                  - text: ) and a representative gene space assembly of cultivar SP80‐3280 (Souza et al.,
                  - link "2019" [ref=e124] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-1001"
                  - text: ). The close diploid relative,
                  - emphasis [ref=e125]: Sorghum bicolor
                  - text: ", provides pan‐genomic resources for translational genomics, with studies showing 95.5% sequence identity between a bacterial artificial chromosome (BAC) library of sugarcane cultivar R570 and the sorghum reference genome (Federico et al.,"
                  - link "2022" [ref=e126] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0049"
                  - text: ; Figueira et al.,
                  - link "2012" [ref=e127] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0051"
                    - superscript [ref=e128]: "2012"
                  - text: ; Wang et al.,
                  - link "2010" [ref=e129] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0214"
                    - superscript [ref=e130]: "2010"
                  - text: ). These resources have facilitated research into economically relevant traits such as sucrose accumulation, lignin biosynthesis, biomass oil accumulation, and stress tolerance (Khan,
                  - link "2021" [ref=e131] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0095"
                  - text: ; Laksana et al.,
                  - link "2024" [ref=e132] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0106"
                    - superscript [ref=e133]: "2024"
                  - text: ; Mehdi et al.,
                  - link "2024" [ref=e134] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0133"
                    - superscript [ref=e135]: "2024"
                  - text: ; Qin et al.,
                  - link "2021" [ref=e136] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0168"
                    - superscript [ref=e137]: "2021"
                  - text: ).
                - paragraph [ref=e138]:
                  - text: Modern sugarcane cultivars inherit 70–80% of their genome from
                  - emphasis [ref=e139]: S. officinarum
                  - text: and 20–25% from
                  - emphasis [ref=e140]: S. spontaneum
                  - text: ", with 5–10% comprising recombinant chromosomes (Garsmeur et al.,"
                  - link "2018" [ref=e141] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0060"
                  - text: ; Healey et al.,
                  - link "2024" [ref=e142] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0070"
                    - superscript [ref=e143]: "2024"
                  - text: ; Mudge et al.,
                  - link "2009" [ref=e144] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0144"
                    - superscript [ref=e145]: "2009"
                  - text: ). Energycane cultivars, with a higher proportion of the
                  - emphasis [ref=e146]: S. spontaneum
                  - text: genome, exhibit increased biomass, tiller number, fiber content, and persistence, but reduced sugar content and stem diameter (Matsuoka et al.,
                  - link "2014" [ref=e147] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0131"
                  - text: ). The expanding availability of genomic resources for this most complex of all crop genomes is enabling novel genetic modification technologies, including transgene overexpression, RNAi, and gene editing. However, challenges persist in transformation, target selection, high genetic redundancy, and mutant screening (Brant, May, et al.,
                  - link "2024" [ref=e148] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0024"
                  - text: ; May et al.,
                  - link "2023" [ref=e149] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0132"
                    - superscript [ref=e150]: "2023"
                  - text: ). The highly polyploid nature of the sugarcane genome typically requires modification of all gene copies/alleles to generate a “loss of function” phenotype for the target locus. This requires highly efficient transformation and editing tools, as well as extensive screening of mutants (May et al.,
                  - link "2023" [ref=e151] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0132"
                  - text: ). While the highly polyploid genome presents challenges for complete target locus modification, it also offers advantages for fine‐tuning of the editing outcomes depending on the number of copies/alleles that are co‐edited (Brant, Eid, et al.,
                  - link "2024" [ref=e152] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0023"
                  - text: ; Eid et al.,
                  - link "2021" [ref=e153] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0044"
                    - superscript [ref=e154]: "2021"
                  - text: ).
                - paragraph [ref=e155]: RNAi studies, where gene silencing is achieved to similar or varying levels, can provide valuable insight into potential genomic targets for knockouts, as well as the desired extent of allelic co‐editing in sugarcane.
              - generic [ref=e156]:
                - heading "SUGARCANE TRANSFORMATION" [level=2] [ref=e157]: SUGARCANE TRANSFORMATION
                - generic [ref=e158]:
                  - heading "Transformation methods in sugarcane" [level=3] [ref=e159]
                  - paragraph [ref=e160]:
                    - text: The main methods for sugarcane transformation are biolistic gene transfer (BGT) and
                    - emphasis [ref=e161]: Agrobacterium
                    - text: ‐mediated transformation (AMT; Figure
                    - link "1" [ref=e162] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-fig-0001"
                    - text: ). While polyethylene glycol‐mediated protoplast transformation has demonstrated success historically, its poor reproducibility and diminished agronomic performance of regenerated plants due to extended tissue culture periods have limited its practical application (Evans et al.,
                    - link "1980" [ref=e163] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0046"
                    - text: ; Larkin,
                    - link "1981" [ref=e164] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0107"
                      - superscript [ref=e165]: "1981"
                    - text: ; Srinivasan & Vasil,
                    - link "1986" [ref=e166] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0195"
                      - superscript [ref=e167]: "1986"
                    - text: ).
                  - figure [ref=e168]:
                    - heading "Figure 1." [level=4] [ref=e169]
                    - paragraph [ref=e170]:
                      - img "Figure 1" [ref=e171]
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e173] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/tpj70048-fig-0001/
                    - generic [ref=e174]:
                      - paragraph [ref=e175]: Strategies for generation, screening, and confirmation of transgene‐free sugarcane plants.
                      - paragraph [ref=e176]: Cas9 RNP and nCas‐base editor RNP systems could be used alongside guide RNAs to generate transgene‐free sugarcane plants. Biolistics and PEG‐mediated transformation are the two available methods for RNP delivery, with biolistic transformation holding the benefit of fewer somaclonal variations and accelerated plant regeneration. Capillary electrophoresis, high‐resolution melt analysis (HRMA), or Cas9 RNP assays could then be used to screen for mutants with non‐selectable phenotypes, followed by Sanger Sequencing or Next Generation Sequencing (NGS) technologies to confirm mutation sequence and size. A selectable phenotype can be generated via nCas base editor‐mediated precision nucleotide substitutions in genes like acetolactate synthase conferring a selectable herbicide‐resistant phenotype. This will facilitate recovery of co‐edited target genes and streamline the analysis of regenerated plants.
                  - paragraph [ref=e177]:
                    - text: BGT utilizes a gene gun to deliver DNA‐coated micro‐particles of tungsten or gold into cell cultures (Altpeter & Sandhu,
                    - link "2010" [ref=e178] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0008"
                    - text: ; Klein et al.,
                    - link "1987" [ref=e179] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0098"
                      - superscript [ref=e180]: "1987"
                    - text: ). While traditional protocols used large quantities of whole plasmid DNA, modern approaches employ minimal cassettes, resulting in lower copy numbers and improved transgene expression stability without undesired backbone DNA integration (Breitler et al.,
                    - link "2002" [ref=e181] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0025"
                    - text: ; Fu et al.,
                    - link "2000" [ref=e182] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0054"
                      - superscript [ref=e183]: "2000"
                    - text: ; Lowe et al.,
                    - link "2009" [ref=e184] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0121"
                      - superscript [ref=e185]: "2009"
                    - text: ; Sandhu & Altpeter,
                    - link "2008" [ref=e186] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0181"
                      - superscript [ref=e187]: "2008"
                    - text: ). BGT's advantages include the ability to simultaneously deliver multiple unlinked constructs with flexible host or tissue requirements due to its mechanical nature (Altpeter et al.,
                    - link "2005" [ref=e188] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0005"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "2016" [ref=e189] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0009"
                    - text: ; Lacroix & Citovsky,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e190] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0104"
                      - superscript [ref=e191]: "2020"
                    - text: ). However, the method can cause tissue and chromosome damage, along with transgene fragmentation when using high DNA and particle quantities (Liu et al.,
                    - link "2019" [ref=e192] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0115"
                    - text: ). Therefore, careful optimizations of tissue culture conditions, DNA concentration and quality, biolistic parameters, and post‐bombardment selection are necessary (Taparia, Gallo & Altpeter,
                    - link "2012" [ref=e193] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0202"
                    - text: ; Budeguer et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e194] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0027"
                      - superscript [ref=e195]: "2021"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e196]:
                    - text: The first successful BGT demonstration in sugarcane by Bower and Birch in 1992, which was followed by optimization for herbicide resistance through
                    - emphasis [ref=e197]: bar
                    - text: gene expression (Gallo‐Meagher & Irvine,
                    - link "1996" [ref=e198] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0057"
                    - text: ), established a foundation that continues to influence current research directions and applications.
                  - paragraph [ref=e199]:
                    - text: AMT leverages
                    - emphasis [ref=e200]: Agrobacterium tumefaciens'
                    - text: natural ability to transfer DNA into host genomes. First demonstrated in sugarcane in 1998 with the
                    - emphasis [ref=e201]: gusA
                    - text: gene (Arencibia et al.,
                    - link "1998" [ref=e202] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0011"
                    - text: ) and subsequently with the
                    - emphasis [ref=e203]: bar
                    - text: gene (Enríquez‐Obregón et al.,
                    - link "1998" [ref=e204] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0045"
                    - text: ), AMT typically produces lower transgene copy numbers and reduced fragmentation, facilitating regulatory approval (Jackson et al.,
                    - link "2013" [ref=e205] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0076"
                    - text: ). However, the method shows limitations for co‐delivering multiple constructs and exhibits high genotype dependency in monocot species (Rahman et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e206] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0171"
                    - text: ; Shrawat & Lörz,
                    - link "2006" [ref=e207] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0189"
                      - superscript [ref=e208]: "2006"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e209]:
                    - text: Comparative studies have revealed the relative strengths of both methods. Jackson et al. (
                    - link "2013" [ref=e210] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0076"
                    - text: ) demonstrated comparable transformation efficiencies between AMT and low‐DNA BGT in sugarcane genotype Q117, though BGT events exhibited superior field performance (Joyce et al.,
                    - link "2013" [ref=e211] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-1002"
                    - text: ). A comprehensive study by Wu et al. (
                    - link "2015" [ref=e212] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0224"
                    - text: ) across 12 experiments with the CP88‐1762 genotype found no significant differences between methods in transformation efficiency, single copy integration frequency, or transgene expression stability. While Jung and Altpeter (
                    - link "2016" [ref=e213] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0082"
                    - text: ) observed higher editing efficiency with AMT (74%) compared to BGT (30%) with transcription activator‐like effector nucleases (TALEN), subsequent field trials again showed better agronomic performance of BGT events (Kannan et al.,
                    - link "2018" [ref=e214] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0088"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e215]:
                    - text: Recent research has focused on addressing AMT's genotype limitations in sugarcane, with progress in optimization and expanded applications (Mohan et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e216] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0139"
                    - text: ).
                - generic [ref=e217]:
                  - heading "Sugarcane tissue culture and regeneration of transgenic cells" [level=3] [ref=e218]
                  - paragraph [ref=e219]:
                    - text: Efficient sugarcane transformation depends on reliable tissue culture protocols that enable the production of totipotent, de‐differentiated cells from meristematic explants capable of plant regeneration post‐transformation. These cultures are initiated and maintained on media containing varying levels of phytohormones (Chen et al.,
                    - link "1988" [ref=e220] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0033"
                    - text: ; Chengalrayan & Gallo‐Meagher,
                    - link "2001" [ref=e221] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0034"
                      - superscript [ref=e222]: "2001"
                    - text: "). Two primary routes exist for inducing embryogenesis: direct somatic embryogenesis (DSE), where embryos form directly from isolated tissues (Shah et al.,"
                    - link "2009" [ref=e223] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0187"
                    - text: ; Taparia, Fouad, et al.,
                    - link "2012" [ref=e224] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0202"
                      - superscript [ref=e225]: "2012"
                    - text: ; van der Vyver,
                    - link "2010" [ref=e226] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0208"
                      - superscript [ref=e227]: "2010"
                    - text: ), and indirect somatic embryogenesis (ISE), involving callus formation before embryogenesis (Bower & Birch,
                    - link "1992" [ref=e228] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0021"
                    - text: ; Taparia, Gallo, & Altpeter,
                    - link "2012" [ref=e229] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0203"
                      - superscript [ref=e230]: "2012"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e231]:
                    - text: While both methods have proven successful, ISE is generally preferred due to its higher transformation efficiency and lower escape rates, despite requiring longer culture periods (~18 vs. ~12 weeks for DSE) and increased risk of somaclonal variations (Kaeppler et al.,
                    - link "2000" [ref=e232] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0087"
                    - text: ; Snyman et al.,
                    - link "2001" [ref=e233] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0194"
                      - superscript [ref=e234]: "2001"
                    - text: ). Regeneration efficiency depends on multiple factors, including explant type and quality, genotype, medium composition, phytohormone balance, culture duration, and environmental conditions. Most effective protocols utilize either immature inflorescences (Desai et al.,
                    - link "2004" [ref=e235] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0041"
                    - text: ; Joshi et al.,
                    - link "2013" [ref=e236] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0081"
                      - superscript [ref=e237]: "2013"
                    - text: ; Liu,
                    - link "1993" [ref=e238] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0116"
                      - superscript [ref=e239]: "1993"
                    - text: ; Wang, Javed, et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e240] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0218"
                      - superscript [ref=e241]: "2024"
                    - text: ) or leaf whorl cross‐sections (Brant, Eid, et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e242] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0023"
                    - text: ; Taparia, Gallo, & Altpeter,
                    - link "2012" [ref=e243] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0203"
                      - superscript [ref=e244]: "2012"
                    - text: ; Ullah et al.,
                    - link "2016" [ref=e245] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0207"
                      - superscript [ref=e246]: "2016"
                    - text: ). Leaf whorls are often preferred due to year‐round availability from maintained sugarcane stands, whereas immature inflorescences require specific seasonal or controlled growth conditions (Joshi et al.,
                    - link "2013" [ref=e247] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0081"
                    - text: ). Alternative explants like basal or axillary meristems have shown lower transformation efficiencies (Enríquez‐Obregón et al.,
                    - link "1998" [ref=e248] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0045"
                    - text: ; Manickavasagam et al.,
                    - link "2004" [ref=e249] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0128"
                      - superscript [ref=e250]: "2004"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e251]:
                    - text: Compared to its monocot relatives, sugarcane's tissue culture and regeneration protocols are notably more reproducible and less genotype‐dependent. Despite high sequence conservation with sorghum (Figueira et al.,
                    - link "2012" [ref=e252] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0051"
                    - text: ; Wang et al.,
                    - link "2010" [ref=e253] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0214"
                      - superscript [ref=e254]: "2010"
                    - text: ), the latter remains more recalcitrant to tissue culture, with only one routinely transformable genotype, Tx430 (Parikh et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e255] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0160"
                    - text: ). Similar limitations exist in maize (Kausch et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e256] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0093"
                    - text: ), wheat (Lee et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e257] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0109"
                    - text: ), rye (Popelka et al.,
                    - link "2003" [ref=e258] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0163"
                    - text: ), barley (Fang et al.,
                    - link "2002" [ref=e259] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0047"
                    - text: ), and switchgrass (Xu et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e260] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0226"
                    - text: ), where tissue culture recalcitrance restricts experiments to few amenable genotypes (Long et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e261] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0118"
                    - text: ; Lowe et al.,
                    - link "2016" [ref=e262] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0123"
                      - superscript [ref=e263]: "2016"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e264]:
                    - text: A breakthrough came in 2016 when Lowe et al. demonstrated that overexpressing morphogenic genes
                    - emphasis [ref=e265]: BABY BOOM
                    - text: (
                    - emphasis [ref=e266]: BBM
                    - text: ) and
                    - emphasis [ref=e267]: WUSCHEL2
                    - text: (
                    - emphasis [ref=e268]: WUS2
                    - text: ) could overcome this recalcitrance in multiple crops, achieving up to 40% transformation efficiency in previously un‐transformable genotypes. This approach has been refined for various crops and explant types (Johnson et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e269] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0080"
                    - text: ; Nelson‐Vasilchik et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e270] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0149"
                      - superscript [ref=e271]: "2022"
                    - text: ; Wang et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e272] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0216"
                      - superscript [ref=e273]: "2023"
                    - text: ; Xu et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e274] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0226"
                      - superscript [ref=e275]: "2022"
                    - text: ). Other successful morphogenic genes include
                    - emphasis [ref=e276]: GROWTH‐REGULATING FACTOR 4
                    - text: and its cofactor
                    - emphasis [ref=e277]: GRF‐INTERACTING FACTOR 1
                    - text: (Debernardi et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e278] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0040"
                    - text: ), as well as
                    - emphasis [ref=e279]: GROWTH‐REGULATING FACTOR 5
                    - text: ","
                    - emphasis [ref=e280]: KNOTTED 1
                    - text: ","
                    - emphasis [ref=e281]: WUSCHEL‐RELATED HOMEOBOX 5
                    - text: ","
                    - emphasis [ref=e282]: LEAFY COTYLEDON 1
                    - text: ", and"
                    - emphasis [ref=e283]: LEAFY COTYLEDON 2
                    - text: (Lee & Wang,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e284] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0110"
                    - text: ; Yan et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e285] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0229"
                      - superscript [ref=e286]: "2023"
                    - text: ). However, controlling the expression of morphogenic genes and their removal is typically necessary post‐regeneration to prevent developmental abnormalities (Lowe et al.,
                    - link "2018" [ref=e287] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0122"
                    - text: ) and ensure successful commercialization (Singh et al.,
                    - link "2013" [ref=e288] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0190"
                    - text: ).
                - generic [ref=e289]:
                  - heading "Modes for improving and advancing sugarcane transformation" [level=3] [ref=e290]
                  - paragraph [ref=e291]:
                    - text: While several elite sugarcane genotypes can be transformed with high efficiency, energycane cultivars remain largely recalcitrant to tissue culture (Luo et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e292] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0124"
                    - text: ). The time, resource, and labor‐intensive nature of tissue culture and transformation necessitates continued exploration of novel technologies.
                  - paragraph [ref=e293]:
                    - text: Recent advances in AMT of other monocotyledonous crops show promising potential for enhancing transformation efficiencies in sugarcane. Novel ternary vector systems incorporating additional accessory helper plasmids carrying extra virulence genes have been developed, expanding cargo capacity compared to superbinary vectors (Aliu et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e294] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0004"
                    - text: ). These systems have demonstrated up to 103% transformation efficiency in an elite maize inbred line (Anand et al.,
                    - link "2018" [ref=e295] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0010"
                    - text: ; Zhang et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e296] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0234"
                      - superscript [ref=e297]: "2020"
                    - text: ) and a 7.71‐fold increase in sorghum when combined with
                    - emphasis [ref=e298]: GRF4‐GIF1
                    - text: morphogenic genes (Li et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e299] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0111"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e300]:
                    - text: Further improvements include the development of thymidine auxotrophic
                    - emphasis [ref=e301]: Agrobacterium
                    - text: strains (Aliu et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e302] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0004"
                    - text: ). Ranch et al. (
                    - link "2012" [ref=e303] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0172"
                    - text: ) addressed common overgrowth issues by generating the LBA4404Thy‐ strain through
                    - emphasis [ref=e304]: THYMIDYLATE SYNTHASE
                    - text: gene mutation or removal. This allows reduction of required antibiotic loads post‐cocultivation, when medium supplementation with thymine is discontinued to suppress
                    - emphasis [ref=e305]: Agrobacterium
                    - text: overgrowth. This advancement has been implemented in various strains including AGL1, EHA101, EHA105, and EHA105D, and has also been explored with methionine auxotrophy (Aliu et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e306] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0004"
                    - text: ; Prías‐Blanco et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e307] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0167"
                      - superscript [ref=e308]: "2022"
                    - text: ; Tamzil et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e309] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0200"
                      - superscript [ref=e310]: "2021"
                    - text: ).
              - generic [ref=e311]:
                - heading "SUGARCANE GENOME MODIFCATION" [level=2] [ref=e312]: SUGARCANE GENOME MODIFCATION
                - generic [ref=e313]:
                  - heading "Over expression transgenesis" [level=3] [ref=e314]
                  - paragraph [ref=e315]:
                    - text: In terms of genome modification, overexpression (OE) transgenesis has successfully enhanced sugarcane's biotic and abiotic stress tolerance. Recent comprehensive reviews by Budeguer et al. (
                    - link "2021" [ref=e316] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0027"
                    - text: ) and Dinesh Babu et al. (
                    - link "2022" [ref=e317] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0042"
                    - text: ) detail these improvements. Sugarcane's vegetative propagation capability provides significant advantages for multi‐gene OE studies and gene stacking through re‐transformation, as it prevents transgene segregation and maintains initial genetic backgrounds (Altpeter & Karan,
                    - link "2018" [ref=e318] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0006"
                    - text: ). The absence of viable pollen and seed production under typical growing conditions enhances transgenic plant containment and biosafety during field testing (Altpeter & Oraby,
                    - link "2010" [ref=e319] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0007"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e320]:
                    - text: Given sugarcane's importance as a biofuel feedstock, significant research has focused on reducing lignocellulosic biomass recalcitrance to saccharification for bio‐ethanol production (reviewed by Budeguer et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e321] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0027"
                      - superscript [ref=e322]: "2021"
                    - text: ; Dinesh Babu et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e323] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0042"
                      - superscript [ref=e324]: "2022"
                    - text: ) and metabolic engineering for increased biomass oil or biomass production. Notable success has been achieved in triacylglycerol (TAG) hyper‐accumulation through overexpression of lipogenic genes like
                    - emphasis [ref=e325]: WRINKLED1
                    - text: (
                    - emphasis [ref=e326]: WRI1
                    - text: ),
                    - emphasis [ref=e327]: OLEOSIN
                    - text: (
                    - emphasis [ref=e328]: OLE
                    - text: ), and
                    - emphasis [ref=e329]: DIACYLGLYCEROL ACYLTRANSFERASE
                    - text: (
                    - emphasis [ref=e330]: DGAT
                    - text: ), combined with TAG hydrolysis suppression via RNAi (Cao, Kannan, et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e331] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0030"
                    - text: ; Cao, Luo, et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e332] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0031"
                      - superscript [ref=e333]: "2023"
                    - text: ; Luo et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e334] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0124"
                      - superscript [ref=e335]: "2022"
                    - text: ; Parajuli et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e336] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0159"
                      - superscript [ref=e337]: "2020"
                    - text: ; Zale et al.,
                    - link "2016" [ref=e338] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0230"
                      - superscript [ref=e339]: "2016"
                    - text: ). These “oilcane” plants have demonstrated impressive field performance, yielding 0.38 MT/ha of vegetative lipid with 88.7% recovery efficiency, approaching soybean oil yield levels (Cao, Kannan, et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e340] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0030"
                    - text: ; Kannan, Liu, et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e341] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0089"
                      - superscript [ref=e342]: "2022"
                    - text: ; Maitra et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e343] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0126"
                      - superscript [ref=e344]: "2024"
                    - text: ).
                - generic [ref=e345]:
                  - heading "RNA antisense (RNAa) and RNAi" [level=3] [ref=e346]
                  - paragraph [ref=e347]:
                    - text: Gene silencing through RNA antisense (RNAa) and RNAi technologies are evolutionarily conserved mechanisms spanning from algae to animals (Agrawal et al.,
                    - link "2003" [ref=e348] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0002"
                    - text: ; Dana et al.,
                    - link "2017" [ref=e349] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0038"
                      - superscript [ref=e350]: "2017"
                    - text: ). RNAa technology utilizes single‐stranded RNA to trigger degradation of mRNA prior to its translation (Santini et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e351] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0182"
                    - text: ; Tilahun et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e352] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0204"
                      - superscript [ref=e353]: "2021"
                    - text: ). When a plasmid containing a DNA antisense fragment is introduced into a cell, the antisense RNA binds to its complementary “native sense” RNA strand, forming a duplex. This complex is recognized by RNAse H enzyme, which degrades the mRNA‐antisense RNA duplex in the nucleus (Abhary & Rezk,
                    - link "2016" [ref=e354] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0001"
                    - text: ; Bird & Ray,
                    - link "1991" [ref=e355] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0020"
                      - superscript [ref=e356]: "1991"
                    - text: ; Frizzi & Huang,
                    - link "2010" [ref=e357] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0053"
                      - superscript [ref=e358]: "2010"
                    - text: ; Santini et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e359] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0182"
                      - superscript [ref=e360]: "2022"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e361]:
                    - text: The first commercial application of RNAa in plants was the “FlavrSavr” tomato, which used an antisense copy of
                    - emphasis [ref=e362]: POLYGLACTURONASE
                    - text: (
                    - emphasis [ref=e363]: PG
                    - text: ) to reduce cell wall degradation during ripening (Frizzi & Huang,
                    - link "2010" [ref=e364] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0053"
                    - text: ). Applications of antisense technology in sugarcane include down‐regulation of
                    - emphasis [ref=e365]: "PYROPHOSPHATE: FRUCTOSE 6‐PHOSPHATE 1‐PHOSPHOTRANSFERASE"
                    - text: ", enhancing sucrose accumulation in immature internodes (Groenewald & Botha,"
                    - link "2008" [ref=e366] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0066"
                    - text: ). Wang et al. (
                    - link "2009" [ref=e367] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0212"
                    - text: ) demonstrated an antisense fragment's ability to inhibit the ACC OXIDASE enzyme, which negatively impacted plant development through dwarfism and delayed development. RNAa was also used by Rossouw et al. (
                    - link "2010" [ref=e368] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0178"
                    - text: ) to reduce neutral invertase activity in sugarcane, resulting in an increase in sucrose synthase activity, as well as an increased sucrose‐to‐hexose ratio.
                  - paragraph [ref=e369]:
                    - text: RNAi, in comparison, is more efficient by delivering double‐stranded RNA directly to cells or using RNAi hairpin constructs (Fire et al.,
                    - link "1998" [ref=e370] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0052"
                    - text: ; Waterhouse et al.,
                    - link "1998" [ref=e371] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0220"
                      - superscript [ref=e372]: "1998"
                    - text: ). RNAi plays crucial roles in epigenetic regulation of endogenous genes and preserving genome integrity by eliminating foreign nucleic acids from viruses and transposable elements (Brant & Budak,
                    - link "2018" [ref=e373] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0022"
                    - text: ; Holoch & Moazed,
                    - link "2015" [ref=e374] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0073"
                      - superscript [ref=e375]: "2015"
                    - text: ; Kim & Rossi,
                    - link "2008" [ref=e376] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-1003"
                      - superscript [ref=e377]: "2008"
                    - text: ; Obbard et al.,
                    - link "2009" [ref=e378] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0153"
                      - superscript [ref=e379]: "2009"
                    - text: ). RNAi has been adapted for targeted gene silencing in functional genomics and crop improvement (Dana et al.,
                    - link "2017" [ref=e380] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0038"
                    - text: ; Koeppe et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e381] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0101"
                      - superscript [ref=e382]: "2023"
                    - text: ; Napoli et al.,
                    - link "1990" [ref=e383] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0146"
                      - superscript [ref=e384]: "1990"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e385]:
                    - text: Gene suppression through RNAi can occur via post‐transcriptional gene silencing (PTGS) through RNA destabilization or translation inhibition, or via transcriptional gene silencing (TGS) through DNA methylation (Liu et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e386] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0117"
                    - text: ). The RNAi mechanism involves the processing of double‐stranded RNA (dsRNA) by DICER and ARGONAUTE proteins. In the cytoplasm, DICER cleaves the dsRNA into 20–30 nucleotide sequences. An ARGONAUTE protein then binds to the short interfering RNA (siRNA), forming an RNA‐Induced Silencing Complex (RISC). This complex separates the RNA strands and uses the antisense strand to locate and bind to complementary mRNA. Endonucleolytic cleavage of the target mRNA sequence by the catalytically active ARGONAUTE protein requires a near‐perfect complementary match between the guide strand and target mRNA sequence. Alternatively, a partial sequence match between the guide strand and target mRNA leads to translational repression (Pratt & MacRae,
                    - link "2009" [ref=e387] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0165"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e388]:
                    - text: RNAi technology has been successfully applied to various polyploid plant species, including banana (Dang et al.,
                    - link "2014" [ref=e389] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0039"
                    - text: ; Shekhawat et al.,
                    - link "2012" [ref=e390] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0188"
                      - superscript [ref=e391]: "2012"
                    - text: ), potato (Hameed et al.,
                    - link "2017" [ref=e392] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0069"
                    - text: ; Sun et al.,
                    - link "2016" [ref=e393] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0197"
                      - superscript [ref=e394]: "2016"
                    - text: ), and wheat (Christensen et al.,
                    - link "2004" [ref=e395] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0035"
                    - text: ; Loukoianov et al.,
                    - link "2005" [ref=e396] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0119"
                      - superscript [ref=e397]: "2005"
                    - text: ; Schweizer et al.,
                    - link "2000" [ref=e398] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0184"
                      - superscript [ref=e399]: "2000"
                    - text: ; Yan et al.,
                    - link "2004" [ref=e400] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0228"
                      - superscript [ref=e401]: "2004"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e402]:
                    - text: For RNAi in plants, hairpin constructs (hpRNA) typically require sense/antisense arm lengths of approximately 100 base pairs with high target region homology. However, these can be much longer without compromising efficiency. This is longer than the 21–23 bp siRNA's due to processing needs for DICER (Smith et al.,
                    - link "2000" [ref=e403] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0193"
                    - text: ; Wesley et al.,
                    - link "2001" [ref=e404] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0221"
                      - superscript [ref=e405]: "2001"
                    - text: ). Using an intron as a spacer between sense and antisense arms can elevate silencing efficiency to nearly 100%, likely due to lariat formation during splicing that enhances RNA duplex formation (Wesley et al.,
                    - link "2001" [ref=e406] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0221"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e407]:
                    - text: RNA‐DEPENDENT RNA POLYMERASE amplifies dsRNA in plants, generating secondary small RNAs for systemic RNAi spread (Baulcombe,
                    - link "2007" [ref=e408] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0015"
                    - text: ). This makes RNAi a powerful tool for delivery strategies that do not require stable transgene integration into the host genome (Liu et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e409] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0117"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e410]:
                    - text: However, researchers have reported unintended silencing of off‐target genes during RNAi applications (Grimm,
                    - link "2011" [ref=e411] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0065"
                    - text: ; Senthil‐Kumar & Mysore,
                    - link "2011" [ref=e412] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0185"
                      - superscript [ref=e413]: "2011"
                    - text: ; Xu et al.,
                    - link "2006" [ref=e414] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0227"
                      - superscript [ref=e415]: "2006"
                    - text: ). To minimize these effects and maximize on‐target gene silencing, specialized software tools have been developed to improve precision and effectiveness (Ahmed et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e416] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0003"
                    - text: ).
                  - generic [ref=e417]:
                    - heading "RNAi in sugarcane" [level=4] [ref=e418]
                    - paragraph [ref=e419]:
                      - text: RNAi technology has presented significant opportunities for sugarcane improvement and functional genomics. While the primary challenge of RNAi in sugarcane revolves around identifying conserved target regions capable of silencing multiple gene copies and alleles, recent publication of a reference genome has greatly streamlined this approach (Garcia et al.,
                      - link "2022" [ref=e420] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0059"
                      - text: ; Healey et al.,
                      - link "2024" [ref=e421] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0070"
                        - superscript [ref=e422]: "2024"
                      - text: ; Lawrence & Pikaard,
                      - link "2003" [ref=e423] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0108"
                        - superscript [ref=e424]: "2003"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e425]:
                      - text: Several studies have demonstrated RNAi's potential in sugarcane (Table
                      - link "1" [ref=e426] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-tbl-0001"
                      - text: ). Osabe et al. (
                      - link "2009" [ref=e427] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0155"
                      - text: ) successfully downregulated the
                      - emphasis [ref=e428]: PHYTOENE DESATURASE
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e429]: PDS
                      - text: ) gene, creating photobleached plants by targeting five different alleles using a 498 bp RNAi hairpin construct. This resulted in four independent transgenic lines with reduced
                      - emphasis [ref=e430]: PDS
                      - text: expression.

                            - cell "0–70% ↓" [ref=e727]
                            - cell "Increased sucrose" [ref=e728]
                            - cell "Y" [ref=e729]
                            - cell "Groenewald and Botha (2008)" [ref=e730]:
                              - text: Groenewald and Botha (
                              - link "2008" [ref=e731] [cursor=pointer]:
                                - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0066"
                              - text: )
                          - row "ACO (1‐AMINOCYCLOPROPANE‐1‐CARBOXYLIC ACID OXIDASE) 1000 19/2/2 NR Dwarfed plants N Wang et al. (2009)" [ref=e732]:
                            - cell "ACO (1‐AMINOCYCLOPROPANE‐1‐CARBOXYLIC ACID OXIDASE)" [ref=e733]:
                              - emphasis [ref=e734]: ACO (1‐AMINOCYCLOPROPANE‐1‐CARBOXYLIC ACID OXIDASE)
                            - cell "1000" [ref=e735]
                            - cell "19/2/2" [ref=e736]
                            - cell "NR" [ref=e737]
                            - cell "Dwarfed plants" [ref=e738]
                            - cell "N" [ref=e739]
                            - cell "Wang et al. (2009)" [ref=e740]:
                              - text: Wang et al. (
                              - link "2009" [ref=e741] [cursor=pointer]:
                                - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0212"
                              - text: )
                          - row "Neutral invertase (NI) 1800 NR/2/2 0–52% ↓ Reduced vigor and dwarfed in tissue culture, altered sugar N Rossouw et al. (2010)" [ref=e742]:
                            - cell "Neutral invertase (NI)" [ref=e743]:
                              - emphasis [ref=e744]: Neutral invertase
                              - text: (
                              - emphasis [ref=e745]: NI
                              - text: )
                            - cell "1800" [ref=e746]
                            - cell "NR/2/2" [ref=e747]
                            - cell "0–52% ↓" [ref=e748]
                            - cell "Reduced vigor and dwarfed in tissue culture, altered sugar" [ref=e749]
                            - cell "N" [ref=e750]
                            - cell "Rossouw et al. (2010)" [ref=e751]:
                              - text: Rossouw et al. (
                              - link "2010" [ref=e752] [cursor=pointer]:
                                - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0178"
                              - text: )
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e754] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: table/tpj70048-tbl-0001/
                      - generic [ref=e755]:
                        - paragraph [ref=e757]: N, no; No., number; NR, not reported; Y, yes.
                        - generic [ref=e758]:
                          - superscript [ref=e759]: "*"
                          - paragraph [ref=e760]: RNAi lines that showed an increase in gene expression compared to wild type are not reported here.
                    - paragraph [ref=e761]:
                      - text: Research focusing on biofuel production has been particularly promising. Studies targeting lignin biosynthesis genes like
                      - emphasis [ref=e762]: CAFFEIC ACID O‐METHYLTRANSFERASE
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e763]: COMT
                      - text: ),
                      - emphasis [ref=e764]: "4‐COUMARATE: COENZYME A LIGASE"
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e765]: 4CL
                      - text: ),
                      - emphasis [ref=e766]: or FERULATE 5‐HYDROXYLASE
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e767]: F5H
                      - text: ) reported reduced sugarcane recalcitrance in biofuel production by increasing the fermentable sugar yields from lignocellulosic biomass (Jung et al.,
                      - link "2012" [ref=e768] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0083"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "2013" [ref=e769] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0085"
                      - text: ). The
                      - emphasis [ref=e770]: COMT
                      - text: RNAi construct, targeting a highly conserved region with 95–100% nucleotide identity across multiple sequences, achieved a 97% decrease in gene expression (Jung et al.,
                      - link "2012" [ref=e771] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0083"
                      - text: ). Field trials demonstrated up to 12% total lignin reduction and increased saccharification efficiency between 28 and 32% (Jung et al.,
                      - link "2013" [ref=e772] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0085"
                      - text: ). Transgenic sugarcane with RNAi suppression of
                      - emphasis [ref=e773]: 4CL
                      - text: displayed a reduction of up to 16.5% of total lignin content and improved saccharification efficiencies of 52–76% compared to wild‐type controls in the field (Jung et al.,
                      - link "2016" [ref=e774] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0084"
                      - text: ). These results highlighted for the first time how RNAi can be applied to add value to lignocellulosic feedstocks in biochemical processing for bioethanol production. Bewg et al. (
                      - link "2016" [ref=e775] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0018"
                      - text: ) confirmed that the RNAi suppression of the lignin biosynthetic genes
                      - emphasis [ref=e776]: COMT
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e777]: FERULATE 5‐HYDROXYLASE
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e778]: F5H
                      - text: ), in contrast to
                      - emphasis [ref=e779]: CAFFEOYL‐COA O‐METHYLTRANSFERASE
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e780]: CCoAOMT
                      - text: ) suppression, improved cell wall saccharification without reduction in soluble sugar production.
                    - paragraph [ref=e781]:
                      - text: Viral resistance has been another significant application. Guo et al. (
                      - link "2015" [ref=e782] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0067"
                      - text: ) used an RNAi hairpin to target the coat protein of sorghum mosaic virus, achieving 87.5% resistance. Similarly, Aslam et al. (
                      - link "2018" [ref=e783] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0012"
                      - text: ) developed an RNAi construct against the sugarcane mosaic virus, reducing coat protein expression by 80–90% in different genotypes. Widyaningrum et al. (
                      - link "2021" [ref=e784] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0222"
                      - text: ) further demonstrated that promoter selection significantly impacts resistance, with the
                      - emphasis [ref=e785]: Zea mays Ubiquitin
                      - text: promoter generating 82.3% resistant lines compared to 57.6% with the
                      - emphasis [ref=e786]: CaMV
                      - text: ‐promoter.
                    - paragraph [ref=e787]:
                      - text: Glassop et al. (
                      - link "2017" [ref=e788] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0064"
                      - text: ) investigated the role of SUCROSE TRANSPORTER 1 (SUT1) protein in the transport of sucrose in sugarcane plants. This group identified six
                      - emphasis [ref=e789]: SUT1
                      - text: variants and designed a hairpin construct to target the 3′‐UTR adjacent sequence in the
                      - emphasis [ref=e790]: SUT1
                      - text: native copies. No correlation between the silencing of
                      - emphasis [ref=e791]: SUT1
                      - text: and the accumulation of sucrose was observed (Glassop et al.,
                      - link "2017" [ref=e792] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0064"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e793]:
                      - text: Most recently, Tang et al. (
                      - link "2024" [ref=e794] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0201"
                      - text: ) simultaneously targeted three
                      - emphasis [ref=e795]: TONOPLAST SUGAR TRANSPORTER
                      - text: genes,
                      - emphasis [ref=e796]: TST1
                      - text: ","
                      - emphasis [ref=e797]: TST2b‐1A
                      - text: ", and"
                      - emphasis [ref=e798]: TST2b‐1C
                      - text: ", reducing stem sucrose content by 80% and highlighting these genes' critical role in sugar accumulation."
                    - paragraph [ref=e799]:
                      - text: Metabolic pathway engineering has also benefited from RNAi approaches. Zale et al. (
                      - link "2016" [ref=e800] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0230"
                      - text: ) and Parajuli et al. (
                      - link "2020" [ref=e801] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0159"
                      - text: ) combined RNAi with gene overexpression to manipulate biomass oil accumulation. By suppressing genes like
                      - emphasis [ref=e802]: PEROXISOMAL ABC TRANSPORTER1
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e803]: PAX1
                      - text: ),
                      - emphasis [ref=e804]: ADP‐GLUCOSE PYROPHOSPHORYLASE
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e805]: AGPase
                      - text: ),
                      - emphasis [ref=e806]: SUGAR DEPENDENT1
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e807]: SDP1
                      - text: ), and/or
                      - emphasis [ref=e808]: TRIGALACTOSYL DIACYLGLYCEROL1
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e809]: TGD1
                      - text: ) while overexpressing key metabolic genes, triacylglycerol accumulation increased in leaf tissue up to 400‐fold and in stem tissue up to 72‐fold compared to wild‐type (WT) plants (Parajuli et al.,
                      - link "2020" [ref=e810] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0159"
                      - text: ; Zale et al.,
                      - link "2016" [ref=e811] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0230"
                        - superscript [ref=e812]: "2016"
                      - text: ). Zale et al. (
                      - link "2016" [ref=e813] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0230"
                      - text: ) described high levels of
                      - emphasis [ref=e814]: PXA1
                      - text: (20% of WT) and
                      - emphasis [ref=e815]: AGPase
                      - text: (1% of WT) silencing in the highest TAG accumulating line (Zale et al.,
                      - link "2016" [ref=e816] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0230"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e817]:
                      - text: Hormone regulation represents another innovative application. Neris et al. (
                      - link "2022" [ref=e818] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0150"
                      - text: ) silenced ethylene production genes
                      - emphasis [ref=e819]: 1‐AMINOCYCLOPROPANE‐1‐CARBOXYLIC ACID SYNTHASES 1; 2; 3
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e820]: ScACS1
                      - text: ","
                      - emphasis [ref=e821]: ScACS2
                      - text: ","
                      - emphasis [ref=e822]: ScACS3
                      - text: ), resulting in increased growth, faster lateral germination, and significant physiological changes. Transgenic lines showed a 31% height increase and a 193.6% increase in leaf area compared to wild type.
                    - paragraph [ref=e823]: These diverse applications underscore RNAi's versatility in sugarcane improvement. By enabling targeted gene suppression across various metabolic pathways, viral resistance, and developmental processes, RNAi offers a powerful tool for crop enhancement. The technique's ability to silence multiple gene copies simultaneously makes it particularly valuable for polyploid crops like sugarcane.
                    - paragraph [ref=e824]: The research demonstrates that RNAi can be effectively used for sugarcane improvement and characterization of candidate genes. These outcomes can be used to highlight targets for improving agronomic traits with targeted mutagenesis by gene editing, particularly if used in combination with traditional gene over‐expression.
                - generic [ref=e825]:
                  - heading "Genome editing technologies" [level=3] [ref=e826]
                  - paragraph [ref=e827]: Site‐specific nucleases (SSN) have revolutionized genome modification by introducing double or single‐strand breaks at targeted DNA locations. These molecular scissors include meganucleases (MNs), zinc‐finger nucleases (ZFN), TALENs, and clustered regularly interspaced short palindromic repeats (CRISPR).
                  - paragraph [ref=e828]:
                    - text: MNs, originating from naturally occurring homing endonucleases, recognize and cleave specific 14–40 bp DNA sequences but are limited by specificity and catalytic activity (Smith et al.,
                    - link "2006" [ref=e829] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0192"
                    - text: ). ZFNs combine 4–6 engineered ZF protein domains with a Fok1 endonuclease. ZFN design remains challenging due to potential low on‐target precision and high off‐target activity (Petolino,
                    - link "2015" [ref=e830] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0162"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e831]:
                    - text: TALENs utilize highly modular TAL effector motifs combined with a Fok1 catalytic domain, offering greater design flexibility and targeting specificity than ZFNs (Khan et al.,
                    - link "2017" [ref=e832] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0097"
                    - text: ). CRISPR distinguishes itself by employing RNA‐guided DNA cleavage through customizable 20 bp guide RNAs (gRNAs) directed by Cas endonucleases. This approach provides enhanced target flexibility, simplified system design, and high multiplex targeting potential (Knott & Doudna,
                    - link "2018" [ref=e833] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0099"
                    - text: ; Stella et al.,
                    - link "2017" [ref=e834] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0196"
                      - superscript [ref=e835]: "2017"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e836]:
                    - text: The CRISPR toolbox has been expanded to include precision tools like base editors, prime editors, and CRISPR‐associated transposons, enabling specific nucleotide substitutions and insertions (Molla et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e837] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0143"
                    - text: ; Tou et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e838] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0205"
                      - superscript [ref=e839]: "2023"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e840]:
                    - text: Following DNA cleavage with SSNs, cellular repair mechanisms such as non‐homologous end joining (NHEJ) or homology‐directed repair (HDR) are initiated. NHEJ may induce random mutations (indels), where HDR provides a more precise template‐mediated repair of cleaved DNA (Manova & Gruszka,
                    - link "2015" [ref=e841] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0129"
                    - text: ; Nisa et al.,
                    - link "2019" [ref=e842] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0152"
                      - superscript [ref=e843]: "2019"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e844]:
                    - text: These technologies have revolutionized plant biotechnology and significantly advanced functional genomics and improvement of crops, including polyploids (May et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e845] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0132"
                    - text: ).
                  - generic [ref=e846]:
                    - heading "Application of TALENs in sugarcane" [level=4] [ref=e847]
                    - paragraph [ref=e848]:
                      - text: The complexity of the sugarcane genome has limited site‐specific nuclease (SSN) applications, with no reports using meganucleases or zinc‐finger nucleases. Jung and Altpeter (
                      - link "2016" [ref=e849] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0082"
                      - text: ) pioneered gene editing in sugarcane using TALENs to target the lignin biosynthesis gene
                      - emphasis [ref=e850]: COMT
                      - text: ", building on previous RNAi research demonstrating lignin reduction potential (Table"
                      - link "2" [ref=e851] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-tbl-0002"
                      - text: ; Jung et al.,
                      - link "2012" [ref=e852] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0083"
                        - superscript [ref=e853]: "2012"
                      - superscript [ref=e854]: ","
                      - link "2013" [ref=e855] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0085"
                        - superscript [ref=e856]: "2013"
                      - text: ).
                    - generic [ref=e857]:
                      - heading "Table 2." [level=5] [ref=e858]
                      - paragraph [ref=e860]: Genome editing using TALENs in sugarcane
                      - table [ref=e862]:
                        - rowgroup [ref=e872]:

                            - cell "Biolistics" [ref=e896]

                            - cell "Biolistics & Agrobacterium" [ref=e900]:
                              - paragraph [ref=e901]:
                                - text: Biolistics &

                            - cell "Reduction of lignin (19.7%) and S/G lignin monomer ratio, 43.8% improved saccharification efficiency" [ref=e905]
                            - cell "Y" [ref=e906]
                            - cell "Y" [ref=e907]
                            - cell "Kannan et al. (2018)" [ref=e908]:
                              - text: Kannan et al. (
                              - link "2018" [ref=e909] [cursor=pointer]:
                                - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0088"
                              - text: )
                          - row "Biolistics NR/1/1 107 of 109 Combining COMT‐edited biomass with engineered yeast strain increased ethanol yields by 148%; COMT editing alone increased ethanol yield by 42% Y Y Ko et al. (2018)" [ref=e910]:
                            - cell "Biolistics" [ref=e911]
                            - cell "NR/1/1" [ref=e912]
                            - cell "107 of 109" [ref=e913]
                            - cell "Combining COMT‐edited biomass with engineered yeast strain increased ethanol yields by 148%; COMT editing alone increased ethanol yield by 42%" [ref=e914]:
                              - text: Combining
                              - emphasis [ref=e915]: COMT
                              - text: ‐edited biomass with engineered yeast strain increased ethanol yields by 148%;
                              - emphasis [ref=e916]: COMT
                              - text: editing alone increased ethanol yield by 42%
                            - cell "Y" [ref=e917]
                            - cell "Y" [ref=e918]
