                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e922] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: table/tpj70048-tbl-0002/
                      - paragraph [ref=e925]: N, no; No., number; NR, not reported; Transform., transformation; Y, yes.
                    - paragraph [ref=e926]:
                      - text: In their groundbreaking study, two
                      - emphasis [ref=e927]: COMT
                      - text: variants (
                      - emphasis [ref=e928]: COMTa
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e929]: COMTb
                      - text: ) were identified in the CP88‐1762 WT genotype. TALEN sites were designed in a conserved region of the first exon, with plant transformation performed using AMT or BGT methods. This resulted in 39 and 27 transgenic lines, respectively.
                    - paragraph [ref=e930]:
                      - text: Mutation assessment through capillary electrophoresis and pyrosequencing revealed that up to 99% of WT
                      - emphasis [ref=e931]: COMT
                      - text: sequences were converted to mutant versions, resulting in a 29–32% reduction in lignin content. The S lignin monomer content decreased while hemicellulose content increased (Jung & Altpeter,
                      - link "2016" [ref=e932] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0082"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e933]:
                      - text: A subsequent study by Kannan et al. (
                      - link "2018" [ref=e934] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0088"
                      - text: ) provided further validation, confirming that 107 of 109
                      - emphasis [ref=e935]: COMT
                      - text: copies/alleles were edited in the sugarcane line CB6. This mutated line maintained the same biomass yield as non‐modified sugarcane but demonstrated a 19.5% lignin content reduction and a significantly altered S/G lignin monomer ratio, elevating biomass saccharification efficiency by 38%.
                    - paragraph [ref=e936]:
                      - text: The research expanded with Ko et al. (
                      - link "2018" [ref=e937] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0100"
                      - text: ), who evaluated ethanol production using gene‐edited biomass. When combined with a recombinant xylose‐utilizing yeast strain, bio‐ethanol production increased by 148% compared to non‐modified biomass. Even with a non‐modified yeast strain, the edited biomass elevated bio‐ethanol production by 42%, demonstrating the potential of engineering both feedstock and microorganisms to dramatically improve bioethanol yield from lignocellulosic biomass.
                  - generic [ref=e938]:
                    - heading "Application of CRISPR in sugarcane" [level=4] [ref=e939]
                    - paragraph [ref=e940]:
                      - text: The first reported use of CRISPR‐Cas9 in sugarcane was a proof‐of‐concept study demonstrating the excision of a 5′‐
                      - emphasis [ref=e941]: loxP
                      - text: fragment using Cas9 directed to the site by a matching sgRNA (Table
                      - link "3" [ref=e942] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-tbl-0003"
                      - text: ; Zhao et al.,
                      - link "2021" [ref=e943] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0236"
                        - superscript [ref=e944]: "2021"
                      - text: ). This research compared
                      - emphasis [ref=e945]: Cre/lox
                      - text: site‐specific recombination, which allows transgene removal by inciting recombination between
                      - emphasis [ref=e946]: loxP
                      - text: sites with CRISPR/Cas‐homologous recombination (HR; Gilbertson,
                      - link "2003" [ref=e947] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0062"
                        - superscript [ref=e948]: "2003"
                      - text: ). Interestingly, homology arms as short as 30 bp could initiate error‐free repair by CRISPR/Cas‐induced HR. However, the
                      - emphasis [ref=e949]: Cre
                      - text: system was found to be more efficient than CRISPR/Cas‐HR excision (Zhao et al.,
                      - link "2021" [ref=e950] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0236"
                      - text: ).
                    - generic [ref=e951]:
                      - heading "Table 3." [level=5] [ref=e952]
                      - paragraph [ref=e954]: Genome editing using CRISPR technologies in sugarcane
                      - table [ref=e956]:
                        - rowgroup [ref=e968]:
                          - 'row "Target name Transform. method Cas enzyme/mechanism No. of gRNAs No. of transgenic lines: generated/with edits/with phenotype No. or % of co‐edited gene copies/alleles Observed phenotype Field test Stably edited progeny References" [ref=e969]':
                            - columnheader "Target name" [ref=e970]
                            - columnheader "Transform. method" [ref=e971]
                            - columnheader "Cas enzyme/mechanism" [ref=e972]
                            - columnheader "No. of gRNAs" [ref=e973]
                            - 'columnheader "No. of transgenic lines: generated/with edits/with phenotype" [ref=e974]'
                            - columnheader "No. or % of co‐edited gene copies/alleles" [ref=e975]
                            - columnheader "Observed phenotype" [ref=e976]
                            - columnheader "Field test" [ref=e977]
                            - columnheader "Stably edited progeny" [ref=e978]
                            - columnheader "References" [ref=e979]

                          - row "loxP site from pYZLoxGus plasmid Biolistics Cas9/NHEJ 1 NR/21/21 NR Error‐free homologous recombination restored reporter gene activity N NR Zhao et al. (2021)" [ref=e981]:
                            - cell "loxP site from pYZLoxGus plasmid" [ref=e982]:
                              - emphasis [ref=e983]: loxP site from pYZLoxGus plasmid
                            - cell "Biolistics" [ref=e984]
                            - cell "Cas9/NHEJ" [ref=e985]
                            - cell "1" [ref=e986]
                            - cell "NR/21/21" [ref=e987]
                            - cell "NR" [ref=e988]
                            - cell "Error‐free homologous recombination restored reporter gene activity" [ref=e989]
                            - cell "N" [ref=e990]
                            - cell "NR" [ref=e991]
                            - cell "Zhao et al. (2021)" [ref=e992]:
                              - text: Zhao et al. (

(2024)" [ref=e1047]:
                            - cell "LG1 (LIGULELESS1)" [ref=e1048]:

                            - cell "78/16/4" [ref=e1053]
                            - cell "Up to 40 of 40 (100%)" [ref=e1054]
                            - cell "25–94% reduction in leaf inclination angle enhanced light penetration into the canopy, increased biomass by 18% (field) to 39% (greenhouse), increased tillering" [ref=e1055]

                                - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0023"

                          - row "MTL (MATRILINEAL) Agrobacterium Cas9/NHEJ 2 30/NR/1 Up to 4 of 6 Decreased anther length and pollen germination rate. Haploid induction in progeny. N Y Guo et al. (2024)" [ref=e1060]:
                            - cell "MTL (MATRILINEAL)" [ref=e1061]:
                              - emphasis [ref=e1062]: MTL (MATRILINEAL)

                              - emphasis [ref=e1064]: Agrobacterium

                            - cell "30/NR/1" [ref=e1067]
                            - cell "Up to 4 of 6" [ref=e1068]
                            - cell "Decreased anther length and pollen germination rate. Haploid induction in progeny." [ref=e1069]
                            - cell "N" [ref=e1070]
                            - cell "Y" [ref=e1071]
                            - cell "Guo et al. (2024)" [ref=e1072]:

                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1075] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: table/tpj70048-tbl-0003/
                      - paragraph [ref=e1078]: dCas9, dead Cas9; HDR, homology‐directed repair; N, no; No., number; NHEJ, non‐homologous end joining; NR, not reported; Transform., transformation; Y, yes.
                    - paragraph [ref=e1079]:
                      - text: Eid et al. (
                      - link "2021" [ref=e1080] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0044"
                      - text: ) achieved the first CRISPR‐mediated mutagenesis of a native gene in sugarcane by targeting the
                      - emphasis [ref=e1081]: MAGNESIUM CHELATASE SUBUNIT I
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1082]: MgCh
                      - text: ). This produced a chlorophyll‐deficient phenotype with leaf colors ranging from green to yellow, depending on the number of co‐edited
                      - emphasis [ref=e1083]: MgCh
                      - text: copies/alleles. Sanger sequencing identified 49 edited copies/alleles, with chlorophyll reduction varying from 20 to 87%. The severity of chlorophyll depletion correlated directly with the number of edited alleles, providing a visual phenotype for optimizing genome editing technologies. Initial optimizations included heat treatments to elevate the editing efficiency of Cas9.
                    - paragraph [ref=e1084]:
                      - text: Oz et al. (
                      - link "2021" [ref=e1085] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0156"
                      - text: ) demonstrated efficient and reproducible targeting of three
                      - emphasis [ref=e1086]: ACETOLACTATE SYNTHASE
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1087]: ALS
                      - text: ) copies conferring herbicide tolerance. Using template‐mediated HDR, they introduced two amino acid substitutions (W574L and S653I) across 11 transgenic lines. Single mutations were also observed, either as W574L or S653I in 25 or 18 lines, respectively (Oz et al.,
                      - link "2021" [ref=e1088] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0156"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e1089]:
                      - text: In 2024, researchers from Thailand used CRISPR/Cas9 to reduce sugarcane lignin content by targeting the
                      - emphasis [ref=e1090]: SoLIM
                      - text: transcription factor. Three gRNAs produced edited lines with 9.74–51.46% reduced lignin content, potentially enhancing the plant's suitability for bioethanol production. Agronomic performance of the events was not reported (Laksana et al.,
                      - link "2024" [ref=e1091] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0106"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e1092]:
                      - text: Sugarcane breeding could potentially be accelerated through the adoption of
                      - emphasis [ref=e1093]: in vivo
                      - text: haploid induction technology by targeted mutation of specific genes including
                      - emphasis [ref=e1094]: CENTROMERIC HISTONE3
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1095]: CENH3
                      - text: ),
                      - emphasis [ref=e1096]: PEROXIDASE
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1097]: POD65
                      - text: ),
                      - emphasis [ref=e1098]: PHOSPHOLIPASE D3
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1099]: PLD3
                      - text: ),
                      - emphasis [ref=e1100]: KOKOPELLI
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1101]: KPL
                      - text: ),
                      - emphasis [ref=e1102]: DOMAIN OF UNKNOWN FUNCTION 679 membrane protein
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1103]: DMP
                      - text: ), and
                      - emphasis [ref=e1104]: MATRILINEAL/PHOSPHOLIPASE A1/NOT LIKE DAD
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1105]: MTL/PLA1/NLD
                      - text: ). Targeted loss of function co‐editing of 4 sugarcane
                      - emphasis [ref=e1106]: MATRILINEAL
                      - text: gene (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1107]: ScMTL
                      - text: ) copies/alleles, led to a haploid induction rate of 0.59–0.96%. Higher haploid induction rates may be achieved by co‐editing of a larger number of
                      - emphasis [ref=e1108]: ScMTL
                      - text: copies (Guo et al.,
                      - link "2024" [ref=e1109] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0068"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e1110]:
                      - text: A groundbreaking study by Brant, Eid, et al. (
                      - link "2024" [ref=e1111] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0023"
                      - text: ) reported the first field trials of CRISPR‐edited sugarcane. The researchers modified leaf inclination angle (LIA) by targeting
                      - emphasis [ref=e1112]: LIGULELESS 1
                      - text: . This holds great potential for enhancing crop yield through increased light capture in the tall sugarcane canopy. Next‐generation sequencing revealed co‐mutation frequencies of 7.4–100% across 40
                      - emphasis [ref=e1113]: LG1
                      - text: copies/alleles. An intermediately edited line with 12% co‐mutation and 54% LIA reduction showed an 18% increase in biomass yield. This demonstrates the advantage of the polyploid genome context for fine‐tuning the editing outcome, allowing elevated crop performance. The authors also confirmed the absence of off‐target mutations (Brant, Eid, et al.,
                      - link "2024" [ref=e1114] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0023"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e1115]: These studies illustrate the tremendous potential of CRISPR‐Cas9 in sugarcane genetic modification, offering precise tools for enhancing agricultural traits and understanding complex genetic mechanisms.
                  - generic [ref=e1116]:
                    - 'heading "dCas9: Transcriptional modulation" [level=4] [ref=e1117]'
                    - paragraph [ref=e1118]:
                      - text: Catalytically inactivated “dead” Cas (dCas) nucleases can bind DNA without cleaving, enabling gene expression regulation in multiple plant species by linking to activation, repressor, methylation, or demethylation domains (Brocken et al.,
                      - link "2018" [ref=e1119] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0026"
                      - text: ; Gjaltema & Rots,
                      - link "2020" [ref=e1120] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0063"
                        - superscript [ref=e1121]: "2020"
                      - text: ; Nakamura et al.,
                      - link "2021" [ref=e1122] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0145"
                        - superscript [ref=e1123]: "2021"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e1124]:
                      - text: In sugarcane, dCas9 systems offer potential for modulating expression of native genes or transgenes. Hooghvorst and Altpeter (
                      - link "2023" [ref=e1125] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0074"
                      - text: ) demonstrated gene suppression by fusing dCas9 with three copies of the SRDX repression domain, targeting the
                      - emphasis [ref=e1126]: MgCh
                      - text: gene. Notably, targeting the 5′UTR achieved the highest average gene repression at 74%, followed by exon 1 at 60% and exon 3 at 47%. This remains the sole report of dCas application in sugarcane to date.
              - generic [ref=e1127]:
                - heading "FUTURE PROSPECTS" [level=2] [ref=e1128]: FUTURE PROSPECTS
                - generic [ref=e1129]:
                  - heading "Novel technologies" [level=3] [ref=e1130]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1131]:
                    - text: Transient delivery of gene‐editing tools through methods like AMT, BGT, or PEG‐mediated protoplast transfection has shown promise for creating transgene‐free edits in various crops. In sugarcane, while these approaches have been employed to study gene functions (Gao et al.,
                    - link "2013" [ref=e1132] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0058"
                    - text: ; Smith et al.,
                    - link "1992" [ref=e1133] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0191"
                      - superscript [ref=e1134]: "1992"
                    - text: ; Wu et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1135] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0225"
                      - superscript [ref=e1136]: "2021"
                    - text: ), their application for generating heritable, transgene‐free edits remains unexplored. Recent innovations in
                    - emphasis [ref=e1137]: Agrobacterium
                    - text: ", such as mutations in the virD2 gene, enable T‐DNA delivery into the nucleus with significantly reduced genomic integration, potentially paving the way for transgene‐free editing (Gelvin & Lee,"
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1138] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0061"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1139]:
                    - text: BGT offers higher transient expression than AMT and is suitable for a broader range of genotypes. To enhance reproducibility, Miller et al. (
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1140] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0136"
                    - text: ) developed a double‐barrel biolistic device coupled with cell‐counting software, allowing simultaneous comparison of samples. This innovation has been employed to optimize BGT parameters and evaluate multiple single‐guide RNAs, showing its utility in refining gene‐editing approaches (Jiang et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1141] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0079"
                    - text: ; Miller et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1142] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0136"
                      - superscript [ref=e1143]: "2021"
                    - superscript [ref=e1144]: ","
                    - link "2022" [ref=e1145] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0137"
                      - superscript [ref=e1146]: "2022"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1147]:
                    - text: Transient RNAi techniques, such as spray‐induced gene silencing (SIGS) and virus‐induced gene silencing (VIGS), have also been explored to identify gene‐editing targets. SIGS involves applying a fine layer of dsRNA onto plant surfaces. Once taken up by the plant cell, it is processed into siRNAs to silence genes without stable transformation (Figure
                    - link "2" [ref=e1148] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-fig-0002"
                    - text: ; Hoang et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e1149] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0071"
                      - superscript [ref=e1150]: "2022"
                    - text: ). This method has shown potential in both monocot and dicot species for target confirmation, pest, and pathogen protection, though challenges like improving the cellular uptake of dsRNAs remain (Hoang et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e1151] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0071"
                    - text: ; Wang & Jin,
                    - link "2017" [ref=e1152] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0215"
                      - superscript [ref=e1153]: "2017"
                    - text: ). Enhancements, such as using siRNAs as structural motifs to synthesize RNA nanoparticles have improved RNAi efficiency and demonstrate the method's evolving capabilities (Zhao et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1154] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0235"
                    - text: ).
                  - figure [ref=e1155]:
                    - heading "Figure 2." [level=4] [ref=e1156]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1157]:
                      - img "Figure 2" [ref=e1158]
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1160] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/tpj70048-fig-0002/
                    - generic [ref=e1161]:
                      - paragraph [ref=e1162]: "RNAi delivery to inform gene editing targets (for example: selecting a lipase target gene for enhancing biomass oil accumulation)."
                      - paragraph [ref=e1163]:
                        - text: (A) Stable transformation involving plant tissue culture and
                        - emphasis [ref=e1164]: in planta
                        - text: delivery. Tissue culture free methods for RNAi (orange lines) or gene editing constructs (black lines) or both RNAi and gene editing constructs (lines with orange and black stripes).
                      - paragraph [ref=e1165]:
                        - text: (B) Type of tissue for transfer of double‐stranded RNA, RNAi expression constructs, or gene editing constructs. For
                        - emphasis [ref=e1166]: in planta
                        - text: methods, reagents are directly applied to plants while stable transformation typically uses callus cultures and their selection and regeneration to plants which is a time‐consuming process.
                      - paragraph [ref=e1167]: "(C) RNAi to identify suitable candidate genes (for example, lipase gene suppression to boost biomass oil accumulation). RNAi technology is used to suppress a candidate gene (such as a specific lipase candidate gene, to enhance lipid accumulation in leaves). The process involves delivering double‐stranded RNA (dsRNA) into cells, which triggers the RNAi pathway as follows: (1) dsRNA delivery via multiple methods (spray, biolistic, viral, or stable transformation) leading to the presence of dsRNA in the plant cell. (1a) RNA‐dependent RNA Polymerase (rdRNA pol): inside the plant cell, the dsRNA can be amplified by the enzyme RNA‐dependent RNA polymerase (rdRNA pol). This boosts the gene‐silencing efficiency by providing additional dsRNA molecules for systemic transfer. (1b) Cell‐to‐cell movement of dsRNA via plasmodesmata: The introduced dsRNA can spread from the initial cell to neighboring cells through plasmodesmata. This leads to systemic spread of the RNAi signal across multiple cells. (2–3) Processing of dsRNA by DICER generates to produce small interfering RNAs (siRNAs). (4) The RNA‐induced silencing complex (RISC), assembles at siRNA's and unwinds the double‐stranded molecule to use one strand in a homology search for the matching target mRNA (for example, a specific lipase mRNA) followed by degradation of the completely homologous target mRNA (step 5). This reduces the production of lipase. (6) Phenotype: suppression of lipase activity leads to lipid accumulation within the leaf cells. The bottom section contrasts this with control leaf cells where RNAi is not applied, allowing normal lipase function and resulting in lipid degradation and low lipid accumulation."
                      - paragraph [ref=e1168]: (D) CRISPR‐Cas9 gene editing for mutagenesis of the target gene informed by RNAi (for example, lipase gene knockout). In the highly polyploid sugarcane CRISPR‐Cas9 gene‐editing can co‐edit variable numbers of copies and alleles of the same target gene. Design of sgRNA's can intentionally include or avoid single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) in the seed sequence of some of the copies/alleles to prevent or support cleavage of all multiple alleles of the lipase gene, respectively. In this example, a single sgRNA is designed to bind a conserved region across most copies/alleles of the lipase gene, aiming to induce mutations in many copies/alleles and reduce overall gene function significantly. However, one of the copies/alleles contains an SNP in the seed sequence of the sgRNA target. The sgRNA‐CRISPR‐Cas9 complex will most likely not cut that specific allele retaining some basic expression level of the targeted lipase. The ability to finetune editing outcomes by copy/allele‐specific design of sgRNAs is a unique opportunity in the genome of highly polyploid species. Sugarcane typically has 10 or more copies/alleles of each target gene. RNAi can inform the desired level of suppression of the target gene. However, achieving a complete loss of function phenotype by gene editing, if desired is far more challenging than in a diploid species.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1169]:
                    - text: VIGS, an RNAi variant, uses viral vectors as a functional genomics method to silence target genes in plants (Figure
                    - link "2" [ref=e1170] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-fig-0002"
                    - text: ; Rossner et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e1171] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0177"
                      - superscript [ref=e1172]: "2022"
                    - text: ). This approach has been successfully implemented in monocots using vectors derived from viruses like barley stripe mosaic virus, brome mosaic virus, and foxtail mosaic virus (Kant & Dasgupta,
                    - link "2019" [ref=e1173] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0092"
                    - text: ). Sugarcane‐specific studies are lacking so far, but the successful use of sugarcane mosaic virus in maize suggests potential applications (Chung et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1174] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0036"
                    - text: ). Viral vectors are modified by inserting gene fragments into non‐essential regions of the viral genome to ensure that the virus can still replicate and move within the host plant. The target gene fragment can be inserted in either sense or antisense orientation or as a hairpin. A recent report on VIGS in rice indicated the highest silencing efficiency for anti‐sense orientation, followed by hairpin and then sense orientation (Kant & Dasgupta,
                    - link "2017" [ref=e1175] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0091"
                    - text: ). VIGS systems have size limitations for insertion of recombinant DNA due to vector stability, replication efficiency, and systemic movement. The ideal insert size is typically in the range of 200–400 bp. Various inoculation methods can be used, including biolistic delivery, rubbing, spraying, and injection techniques (Kant & Dasgupta,
                    - link "2019" [ref=e1176] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0092"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1177]: Despite limitations in vector stability and insert size, VIGS remains a versatile tool for transient gene silencing.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1178]: While CRISPR/Cas research in sugarcane remains in the early stages, the remarkable results obtained from recent publications incite high expectations for application of this technology in the years to come.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1179]:
                    - text: Advances in precision gene editing, such as base editing (BE) and prime editing, hold significant promise for sugarcane improvement. Traditional editing methods are limited by the imprecision of non‐homologous end joining (NHEJ) and the inefficiency of homology‐directed repair (HDR) (Molla et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1180] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0143"
                    - text: ). BE enables precise base conversions without double‐strand breaks by fusing a deaminase with a catalytically inactive or nickase Cas protein. Cytosine base editors (CBE) convert cytosine to uracil, while adenine base editors (ABE) convert adenine to inosine, achieving targeted base changes with high specificity (Hua et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e1181] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0075"
                    - text: ; Komor et al.,
                    - link "2016" [ref=e1182] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0102"
                      - superscript [ref=e1183]: "2016"
                    - text: ). These technologies have been successfully applied to various crops, including rice, soybean, and wheat (Cai et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e1184] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0029"
                    - text: ; Ren, Sretenovic, Liu, Zhong, et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1185] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0175"
                      - superscript [ref=e1186]: "2021"
                    - text: ; Zhang et al.,
                    - link "2019" [ref=e1187] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0232"
                      - superscript [ref=e1188]: "2019"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1189]:
                    - text: Prime editing offers even greater precision and flexibility by combining a Cas nickase with a reverse transcriptase and an extended prime editing guide RNA (pegRNA). This allows DNA insertions or substitutions without requiring large DNA templates, which often integrate randomly. While most studies on prime editing have focused on diploid species, early success in wheat and potatoes indicates its potential for polyploid crops like sugarcane (Ni et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1190] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0151"
                    - text: ; Veillet et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e1191] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0210"
                      - superscript [ref=e1192]: "2020"
                    - text: ). Although neither BE nor prime editing has been demonstrated in sugarcane, they hold tremendous potential for enhancing editing precision and efficiency.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1193]:
                    - text: Traditional gene‐editing methods involving AMT or BGT often result in random DNA integration, raising concerns about off‐target effects and insertional mutagenesis (Tsanova et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1194] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0206"
                    - text: ; Zhang et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1195] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0233"
                      - superscript [ref=e1196]: "2021"
                    - text: ). DNA‐free approaches using ribonucleoprotein complexes (RNPs) present an attractive alternative. RNPs, comprising pre‐assembled Cas proteins and guide RNAs, are delivered transiently, reducing the risk of genomic integration and streamlining regulatory approval (Menz et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e1197] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0135"
                    - text: ; Woo et al.,
                    - link "2015" [ref=e1198] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0223"
                      - superscript [ref=e1199]: "2015"
                    - text: ). Techniques such as PEG‐mediated protoplast transformation and BGT have been used to deliver RNPs, achieving successful edits in crops like maize, wheat, and potato (Liang et al.,
                    - link "2017" [ref=e1200] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0112"
                    - text: ; Makhotenko et al.,
                    - link "2019" [ref=e1201] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0127"
                      - superscript [ref=e1202]: "2019"
                    - text: ; Svitashev et al.,
                    - link "2016" [ref=e1203] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0199"
                      - superscript [ref=e1204]: "2016"
                    - text: ). However, transgene‐free editing with RNP delivery typically does not allow to create a selectable phenotype, making the identification of edited events a daunting task (Molinari et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1205] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0142"
                    - text: ; Prado et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1206] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0164"
                      - superscript [ref=e1207]: "2024"
                    - text: ). To reduce costs associated with sequencing of a very large number of regenerated events, mutation screening methods such as capillary electrophoresis (CE), Cas9 RNP assays, and high‐resolution melt analysis (HRMA) have been compared in sugarcane. CE was the superior approach (Figure
                    - link "1" [ref=e1208] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-fig-0001"
                    - text: ; Brant, May, et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1209] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0024"
                      - superscript [ref=e1210]: "2024"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1211]:
                    - text: Alternatively, the development of RNP‐based base editors offers the opportunity to generate selectable phenotypes, such as herbicide resistance through precise nucleotide substitutions in the
                    - emphasis [ref=e1212]: ALS
                    - text: gene (Oz et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1213] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0156"
                    - text: ). This approach could simplify the identification of edited sugarcane plants. Given sugarcane's clonal propagation and high heterozygosity, RNP delivery could streamline the production of commercially viable, genetically modified varieties.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1214]:
                    - text: The CRISPR toolkit has been expanded to include diverse Cas nucleases with varying properties, such as PAM recognition and cleavage activity. For example, Cas12a recognizes a different PAM sequence and generates staggered DNA breaks, while engineered nucleases like Cas‐NG and RYCas9 have reduced PAM requirements (Paul & Montoya,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e1215] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0161"
                    - text: ; Ren et al.,
                    - link "2019" [ref=e1216] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0174"
                      - superscript [ref=e1217]: "2019"
                    - text: ; Ren, Sretenovic, Liu, Tang, et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1218] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0176"
                      - superscript [ref=e1219]: "2021"
                    - text: ). Although only Cas9 has been used in sugarcane, introducing alternative nucleases could enhance editing flexibility. Smaller nucleases also hold potential for delivery via plant viruses, enabling tissue culture‐free editing and rapid target screening (Khakhar & Voytas,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1220] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0094"
                    - text: ; Liu et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1221] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0114"
                      - superscript [ref=e1222]: "2024"
                    - text: ). Meanwhile, transgenic plants expressing Cas proteins can facilitate gRNA delivery through viral vectors to expedite editing trials (Banakar et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e1223] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0013"
                    - text: ; Baysal et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1224] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0016"
                      - superscript [ref=e1225]: "2024"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1226]:
                    - text: Fusing orthogonal dCas proteins to transcription activators and repressors will allow simultaneous activation and repression of different sets of genes (Pan et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1227] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0157"
                    - text: ; Vazquez‐Vilar et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1228] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0209"
                      - superscript [ref=e1229]: "2023"
                    - text: ). Multigene, metabolic engineering strategies in sugarcane will benefit from these versatile tools.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1230]:
                    - text: Sugarcane breeding faces challenges due to the limited genetic diversity of the parental germplasm used in breeding programs and its complex polyploid genome. Synthetic
                    - emphasis [ref=e1231]: in planta
                    - text: ‐directed evolution (SDE), which combines iterative editing cycles with selection for desirable traits can be very beneficial in this context (Kababji et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1232] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0086"
                    - text: ; Rao et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1233] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0173"
                      - superscript [ref=e1234]: "2021"
                    - text: ). For instance, SDE has been used in Arabidopsis and rice to develop variants with novel mutations conferring herbicide tolerance (Butt et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e1235] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0028"
                    - text: ; Dong et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1236] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0043"
                      - superscript [ref=e1237]: "2024"
                    - text: ; Wang, Pan, et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1238] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0219"
                      - superscript [ref=e1239]: "2024"
                    - text: ). Sugarcane's genetic redundancy makes it an ideal candidate for SDE, as unmutated alleles can preserve essential functions while mutated alleles acquire new traits.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1240]: In conclusion, transient delivery systems, advanced editing technologies, and innovative strategies like orthogonal synthetic transcription factors and SDE hold tremendous potential to accelerate sugarcane genetic improvement. These approaches promise to overcome traditional breeding limitations and enable precise, efficient, and complex modifications in this commercially important crop.
                - generic [ref=e1241]:
                  - heading "Important target traits for sugarcane improvement" [level=3] [ref=e1242]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1243]:
                    - text: Improving sugarcane biomass production under various stress conditions is crucial for yield stability in predicted climate change scenarios. Genetic improvement strategies must address responses to pests, diseases, drought, flooding, salinity, heat, cold, ozone, shading, high wind speed, and nutrient‐deficient soils (Kumar et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1244] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0103"
                    - text: ; Shabbir et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1245] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0186"
                      - superscript [ref=e1246]: "2021"
                    - text: ; Surya Krishna et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1247] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0198"
                      - superscript [ref=e1248]: "2023"
                    - text: ). Promising approaches to improve sugar and biomass yields include suppressing flowering, altering plant architecture, and improving photosynthetic efficiency (Brant, Eid, et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1249] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0023"
                    - text: ; Kannan, Nguyen, et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e1250] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0090"
                      - superscript [ref=e1251]: "2022"
                    - text: ; Salesse‐Smith et al.,
                    - link "2025" [ref=e1252] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0180"
                      - superscript [ref=e1253]: "2025"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1254]:
                    - text: For improving sucrose accumulation a few genes were so far explored by RNAi and RNAa (Glassop et al.,
                    - link "2017" [ref=e1255] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0064"
                    - text: ; Groenewald & Botha,
                    - link "2008" [ref=e1256] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0066"
                      - superscript [ref=e1257]: "2008"
                    - text: ; Mehdi et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1258] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0133"
                      - superscript [ref=e1259]: "2024"
                    - text: ; Rossouw et al.,
                    - link "2010" [ref=e1260] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0178"
                      - superscript [ref=e1261]: "2010"
                    - text: ; Sachdeva et al.,
                    - link "2011" [ref=e1262] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0179"
                      - superscript [ref=e1263]: "2011"
                    - text: ; Tang et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1264] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0201"
                      - superscript [ref=e1265]: "2024"
                    - text: ) and overexpression approaches with limited success (Khan et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1266] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0096"
                    - text: ). The sucrose metabolism involves several compartments for synthesis translocation, carbon partitioning, accumulation, and degradation (Groenewald & Botha,
                    - link "2008" [ref=e1267] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0066"
                    - text: ; Ma et al.,
                    - link "2000" [ref=e1268] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0125"
                      - superscript [ref=e1269]: "2000"
                    - text: ; Qin et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1270] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0168"
                      - superscript [ref=e1271]: "2021"
                    - text: ). Investigating this intricate process may be accelerated by using orthogonal dCas transcription activators and repressors under inducible promoters (Pan et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1272] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0157"
                    - text: ). A detailed description and table summarizing major sugarcane genes and molecules involved in sucrose metabolism is provided by Khan et al. (
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1273] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0096"
                    - text: ). Engineering the duration of stem maturation can also be explored (Khan et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1274] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0096"
                    - text: ; Qin et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1275] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0168"
                      - superscript [ref=e1276]: "2021"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1277]:
                    - text: Recent research has highlighted the role of 23 microRNAs in sucrose accumulation by regulating carbon‐related transcription factors (Banerjee et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e1278] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0014"
                    - text: ). Future investigations may focus on editing microRNA binding sites to better understand this complex trait. A comprehensive approach combining transgenic, breeding, and gene editing strategies may be necessary to significantly increase sucrose content (Jackson,
                    - link "2005" [ref=e1279] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0077"
                    - text: ; Khan et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1280] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0096"
                      - superscript [ref=e1281]: "2023"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1282]:
                    - text: Biomass oil accumulation represents an emerging target, with significant progress already made in sugarcane and energycane (Cao, Luo, et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1283] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0031"
                    - text: ; Maitra et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1284] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0126"
                      - superscript [ref=e1285]: "2024"
                    - text: ; Parajuli et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e1286] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0159"
                      - superscript [ref=e1287]: "2020"
                    - text: ). Refining this strategy requires a deeper understanding of regulatory elements that direct transgene expression to late stem development (Wang et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1288] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0213"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1289]:
                    - text: The potential for engineering additional co‐products in sugarcane or energycane supports the emerging bioeconomy. By manipulating shikimate or isoprenoid pathways, these high‐biomass crops can yield valuable biochemicals and biofuels for industrial applications (Lin & Eudes,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e1290] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0113"
                    - text: ). Such strategies aim to reduce dependence on petroleum‐derived chemicals and fuels, offering a more sustainable approach to industrial production.
                - generic [ref=e1291]:
                  - heading "Regulatory aspects of gene edited sugarcane" [level=3] [ref=e1292]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1293]:
                    - text: Genetic modification and commercialization of transgenic crop species remains a complex and controversial global topic, with significant developments in regulatory approaches and biotechnological advancements over recent years. The global landscape of genetically modified crop cultivation has expanded substantially, marked by several notable milestones (Beker et al.,
                    - link "2016" [ref=e1294] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0017"
                    - text: ; Bhajan et al.,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e1295] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0019"
                      - superscript [ref=e1296]: "2022"
                    - text: ; Mathur et al.,
                    - link "2017" [ref=e1297] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0130"
                      - superscript [ref=e1298]: "2017"
                    - text: ). In 2018, Brazil achieved a significant breakthrough by approving the world's first GM sugarcane for feed and food cultivation, with varieties CTC20BT and CTC9001BT demonstrating resistance to sugarcane borer (
                    - emphasis [ref=e1299]: Diatraea saccharalis
                    - text: ) and adaptability to diverse environmental conditions (CTNBio,
                    - link "2019" [ref=e1300] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0037"
                      - superscript [ref=e1301]: "2019"
                    - text: ; Lajolo et al.,
                    - link "2021" [ref=e1302] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0105"
                      - superscript [ref=e1303]: "2021"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1304]:
                    - text: Indonesia similarly advanced its agricultural biotechnology sector by approving a GM drought‐resistant sugarcane variety, NXI‐4T, with regulatory approvals based on the premise that downstream processing for sucrose production effectively degrades DNA (Cheavegatti‐Gianotto et al.,
                    - link "2011" [ref=e1305] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0032"
                    - text: ; Gain,
                    - link "2018" [ref=e1306] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0055"
                      - superscript [ref=e1307]: "2018"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1308]:
                    - text: Argentina emerged as a global leader in regulatory guidelines for gene‐edited crops. Its regulatory agency CONABIA was recognized by the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) as “The Only Global Reference Center for Biosafety” in 2014, a designation renewed in 2023 for an additional 4 years (Ministry of Economy of Argentina,
                    - link "2022" [ref=e1309] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0138"
                    - text: ). The Argentinian regulatory model distinguishes between crops with stable genome insertion of foreign DNA and biotech products that are transgene‐free, which are considered to be equivalent to conventionally bred crops (Fernández Ríos et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1310] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0050"
                    - text: ; Schmidt et al.,
                    - link "2020" [ref=e1311] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0183"
                      - superscript [ref=e1312]: "2020"
                    - text: ; Beker et al.,
                    - link "2016" [ref=e1313] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0017"
                      - superscript [ref=e1314]: "2016"
                    - text: ). This approach has been widely adopted by numerous countries, including Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Guatemala, Honduras, Paraguay, Ecuador, Philippines, Kenya, and Nigeria, where most gene‐editing products are eligible for regulatory exemption (Fernández Ríos et al.,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1315] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0050"
                    - text: ; Jenkins et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1316] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0078"
                      - superscript [ref=e1317]: "2023"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1318]: Regulatory frameworks vary internationally, with countries like Canada evaluating transgenic and gene‐edited plant products or those generated by conventional mutagenesis based on their novelty rather than development methods.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1319]:
                    - text: In the United States, the regulation and oversight of gene editing in plants is facilitated by the U.S. Coordinated Framework for Regulation of Biotechnology. The US Department of Agriculture (USDA), the US Environmental Protection Agency (EPA), and the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) collectively regulate the marketing and environmental release of gene‐edited products. The United States has evolved its regulatory stance, with the USDA implementing a revision in May 2020 (SECURE rule) that allowed regulatory exemptions for a very small subset of edits, including a single edit in a diploid or haplotype. Therefore, for most transgene‐free, gene‐edited events, the same regulatory review process was required as for transgenic crops (National Archives,
                    - link "2024a" [ref=e1320] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0147"
                      - superscript [ref=e1321]: 2024a
                    - text: ). A recent amendment to these USDA regulations (November 13th, 2024) would have allowed up to 12 edits, made simultaneously or sequentially in all polyploids, without consultation. One edit was defined as edits to 2 copies of one locus, and it did not matter if the loci were homologous or homoeologous. The type of edit eligible for the exemptions would have included insertions and deletions occurring during cellular DNA break repair, as well as a single nucleotide substitution in the absence of an externally provided repair template (National Archives,
                    - link "2024b" [ref=e1322] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0148"
                      - superscript [ref=e1323]: 2024b
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1324]:
                    - text: On December 2, 2024, the United States District Court issued a summary judgment order in the case of National Family Farm Coalition versus Tom Vilsack (case no. 3:21‐cv‐05695) vacating the entire SECURE rule (issued in May 2020). However, the summary judgment maintained the validity of the confirmation of exemptions, the regulatory status reviews, and permits issued under the SECURE rule. The court decision returns the USDA APHIS regulation that existed prior to May 2020. Under those regulations, APHIS had established the “Am I regulated” process, used by many developers of gene‐edited and transgenic plants, to determine the regulated status of modified organisms. Following inquiry, a non‐regulated status is granted if no “plant pest” donor organism, recipient organism, vectors, or vector agents were used in the generation of transgenic or gene‐edited organism. This rule is now in place again and favors the use of biolistic transformation over
                    - emphasis [ref=e1325]: Agrobacterium
                    - text: ‐mediated gene transfer since the latter is considered a plant pest vector.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1326]:
                    - text: The EPA regulates substances that are pesticidal or are plant regulators that are introduced into genetically modified plants. These are considered a “Plant Incorporated Protectant” (PIP) and are treated and regulated like a pesticide. In 2023, the EPA amended its regulations to include two exemption categories for PIPs, one for loss of function PIPs and one for PIPs created through genetic engineering from a sexually compatible plant. EPA established processes for developers to notify and confer with EPA on these types of exempted PIPs (Mendelsohn et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1327] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0134"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1328]:
                    - text: The FDA recently issued guidance for industry describing how developers of gene‐edited plants can voluntarily engage with the FDA. Consultation with the FDA is strongly encouraged if there is any potential for allergenic proteins to be present (FDA,
                    - link "2024" [ref=e1329] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0048"
                      - superscript [ref=e1330]: "2024"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1331]:
                    - text: Other countries such as Australia, Japan, England, and India have developed regulatory systems that provide exemptions for transgene‐free genome edits causing loss of function through natural DNA repair mechanisms, regardless of plant ploidy or gene copy number (Jenkins et al.,
                    - link "2023" [ref=e1332] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tpj70048-bib-0078"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e1333]: The emerging global consensus underscores the current importance of developing strategies for transgene‐free editing, which not only reduces regulatory complexities and associated costs but also accelerates cultivar development. As biotechnological capabilities continue to advance, many regulatory frameworks are adapting to balance innovation, safety, and agricultural productivity.
              - generic [ref=e1334]:
                - heading "AUTHOR CONTRIBUTIONS" [level=2] [ref=e1335]: AUTHOR CONTRIBUTIONS
                - paragraph [ref=e1336]: FA acquired funding, conceived the idea, directed the manuscript content and structure, and edited the manuscript. EB and EZ‐S wrote and contributed equally to the manuscript and should be considered joint first authors. All authors read and approved the final manuscript.
              - generic [ref=e1337]:
                - heading "CONFLICT OF INTEREST" [level=2] [ref=e1338]: CONFLICT OF INTEREST
                - paragraph [ref=e1339]: The authors have not declared a conflict of interest.
              - generic [ref=e1340]:
                - heading "ACKNOWLEDGMENTS" [level=2] [ref=e1341]: ACKNOWLEDGMENTS
                - paragraph [ref=e1342]: The authors would like to thank Wayne Parrott, University of Georgia for a discussion of regulatory aspects of gene‐edited plants and Aiman Ehsan at the University of Florida for carefully compiling the reference list. This review was funded by the DOE Center for Advanced Bioenergy and Bioproducts Innovation (U.S. Department of Energy, Office of Science, Biological and Environmental Research Program under Award Number DE‐SC0018420). Any opinions, findings, conclusions, or recommendations expressed in this publication are those of the author(s) and do not necessarily reflect the views of the U.S. Department of Energy.
              - generic [ref=e1343]:
                - heading "DATA AVAILABILITY STATEMENT" [level=2] [ref=e1344]: DATA AVAILABILITY STATEMENT
                - paragraph [ref=e1345]: Data sharing is not applicable to this article as no new data were created or analyzed in this study.
              - generic [ref=e1346]:
                - heading "References" [level=2] [ref=e1347]: References
                - list [ref=e1349]:
                  - listitem [ref=e1350]:
                    - text: "Abhary, M. & Rezk, A. (2016) RNAi technology: a potential tool in plant breeding. In: Al‐Khayri, J. , Jain, S. & Johnson, D. (Eds.) Advances in plant breeding strategies: breeding, biotechnology and molecular tools, Vol. 1. Cham: Springer, pp. 1–656. Available from: 10.1007/978-3-319-22521-0_14 ["
                    - link "External, opens in a new tab. DOI" [ref=e1351] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-22521-0_14
                    - text: "] ["
                    - link "External, opens in a new tab. Google Scholar" [ref=e1352] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?title=Advances%20in%20plant%20breeding%20strategies:%20breeding,%20biotechnology%20and%20molecular%20tools&author=M.%20Abhary&author=A.%20Rezk&publication_year=2016&
                    - text: "]"
                  - listitem [ref=e1353]:
                    - text: "Agrawal, N. , Dasaradhi, P.V.N. , Mohmmed, A. , Malhotra, P. , Bhatnagar, R.K. & Mukherjee, S.K. (2003) RNA interference: biology, mechanism, and applications. Microbiology and Molecular Biology Reviews, 67, 657–685. Available from: 10.1128/MMBR.67.4.657-685.2003 ["
                    - link "External, opens in a new tab. DOI" [ref=e1354] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://doi.org/10.1128/MMBR.67.4.657-685.2003
                    - text: "] ["
                    - link "PMC free article" [ref=e1355] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: /articles/PMC309050/
                    - text: "] ["
                    - link "PubMed" [ref=e1356] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/14665679/
